Note\nthat we focus on the relative performance change\nbefore and after quantization but not the absolute\nvalue.\nActivation smoothing.\nThe migration strength\nα = 0.5 is a general sweet spot for all the OPT\nand BLOOM models, and α = 0.75 for GLM-\n130B since its activations are more difﬁcult to quan-\ntize (Zeng et al., 2022). We get a suitable α by run-\nning a quick grid search on a subset of the Pile (Gao\net al., 2020) validation set. To get the statistics of\nactivations, we calibrate the smoothing factors and\nthe static quantization step sizes once with 512 ran-\n*https://github.com/EleutherAI/lm-evaluation-harness\n†https://github.com/THUDM/GLM-130B\ndom sentences from the pre-training dataset Pile,\nand apply the same smoothed and quantized model\nfor all downstream tasks. In this way, we can bench-\nmark the generality and zero-shot performance of\nthe quantized LLMs.\nImplementation.\nWe implement SmoothQuant\nwith two backends: (1) PyTorch Huggingface‡ for\nthe proof of concept, and (2) FasterTransformer§,\nas an example of a high-performance framework\nused in production environments. In both PyTorch\nHuggingface and FasterTransformer frameworks,\nwe implement INT8 linear modules and the batched\nmatrix multiplication (BMM) function with CUT-\nLASS INT8 GEMM kernels. We simply replace\nthe original ﬂoating point (FP16) linear modules\nand the bmm function with our INT8 kernels as the\nINT8 model.\n4.2\nAccurate Quantization\nResults of OPT-175B.\nSmoothQuant can handle\nthe quantization of very large LLMs, whose acti-\nvations are more difﬁcult to quantize. We study\nquantization on OPT-175B. As shown in Table 4,\nSmoothQuant can match the FP16 accuracy on all\nevaluation datasets with all quantization schemes.\nLLM.int8() can match the ﬂoating point accu-\nracy because they use ﬂoating-point values to rep-\nresent outliers, which leads to a large latency over-\nhead (Table 6). The W8A8, ZeroQuant, and Outlier\nSuppression baselines produce nearly random re-\nsults, indicating that naively quantizing the activa-\ntion of LLMs will destroy the model performance.\n‡https://github.com/huggingface/transformers\n§https://github.com/NVIDIA/FasterTransformer\n\n\n=== PDF page 7 ===\nFigure 6: SmoothQuant-O3 (the most efﬁcient setting,\ndeﬁned in Table 3) preserves the accuracy of OPT mod-\nels across different scales when quantized to INT8.\nLLM.int8() requires FP16/INT8 mixed precision and\nsuffers from slow down.\nResults of different LLMs.\nSmoothQuant can\nbe applied to various LLM designs. In Table 5, we\nshow SmoothQuant can losslessly quantize all ex-\nisting open LLMs beyond 100B parameters. Com-\npared with the OPT-175B model, the BLOOM-\n176B model is easier to quantize: none of the\nbaselines completely destroys the model; even the\nnaive W8A8 per-tensor dynamic quantization only\ndegrades the accuracy by 4%. The O1 and O2\nlevels of SmoothQuant successfully maintain the\nﬂoating point accuracy, while the O3 level (per-\ntensor static) degrades the average accuracy by\n0.8%, which we attribute to the discrepancy be-\ntween the statically collected statistics and the real\nevaluation samples’ activation statistics. On the\ncontrary, the GLM-130B model is more difﬁcult to\nquantize (which echos Zeng et al.). Nonetheless,\nSmoothQuant-O1 can match the FP16 accuracy,\nwhile SmoothQuant-O3 only degrades the accu-\nracy by 1%, which signiﬁcantly outperforms the\nbaselines. Note that we clip the top 2% tokens\nwhen calibrating the static quantization step sizes\nfor GLM-130B following Wei et al. (2022). It is\ninteresting to see that different model/training de-\nsigns have different quantization difﬁculties, which\nwe hope will inspire future LLM research.\nResults on LLMs of different model sizes.\nSmoothQuant works not only for the very large\nLLMs beyond 100B parameters, but it also works\nconsistently for smaller LLMs. In Figure 6, we\nshow that SmoothQuant can work on all scales of\nOPT models, matching the FP16 accuracy with\nINT8 quantization.\n4.3\nSpeedup and Memory Saving\nIn this section, we show the measured speedup and\nmemory saving of SmoothQuant-O3 integrated into\nPyTorch and FasterTransformer. We measure the\nend-to-end latency of generating all hidden states\nfor a batch of 4 sentences in one pass, i.e., the con-\ntext stage latency. We record the (aggregated) peak\nGPU memory usage in this process. We only com-\npare SmoothQuant with LLM.int8() because\nit is the only existing quantization method that\ncan preserve LLM accuracy at all scales. Due to\nthe lack of support for model parallelism in Hug-\ngingface, we only measure SmoothQuant’s perfor-\nmance on a single GPU for the PyTorch imple-\nmentation, so we choose OPT-6.7B, OPT-13B, and\nOPT-30B for evaluation. In the FasterTransformer\nlibrary, SmoothQuant can seamlessly work with\nTensor Parallelism (Shoeybi et al., 2019) algorithm,\nso we test SmoothQuant on OPT-13B, OPT-30B,\nOPT-66B, and OPT-175B for both single and multi-\nGPU benchmarks. All our experiments are con-\nducted on NVIDIA A100 80GB GPU servers.\nResults of the PyTorch implementation.\nIn\nFigure 7, we show the inference latency and peak\nmemory usage based on the PyTorch implemen-\ntation. SmoothQuant is consistently faster than\nthe FP16 baseline, getting a 1.51x speedup on\nOPT-30B when the sequence length is 256. We\ncan also see a trend that the larger the model, the\nmore signiﬁcant the acceleration. On the other\nhand, LLM.int8() is almost always slower than\nthe FP16 baseline, which is due to the large over-\nhead of the mixed-precision activation represen-\ntation. In terms of memory, SmoothQuant and\nLLM.int8() can all nearly halve the memory us-\nage of the FP16 model, while SmoothQuant saves\nslightly more memory because it uses fully INT8\nGEMMs.\nResults of the FasterTransformer implementa-\ntion.\nAs shown in Figure 8 (top), compared to\nFasterTransformer’s FP16 implementation of OPT,\nSmoothQuant-O3 can further reduce the execu-\ntion latency of OPT-13B and OPT-30B by up to\n1.56× when using a single GPU. This is challeng-\ning since FasterTransformer is already more than\n3× faster compared to the PyTorch implementa-\ntion for OPT-30B. Remarkably, for bigger models\nthat have to be distributed across multiple GPUs,\nSmoothQuant achieves similar or even better la-\ntency using only half the number of GPUs (1 GPU\n\n\n=== PDF page 8 ===\nFigure 7: The PyTorch implementation of SmoothQuant-O3 achieves up to 1.51× speedup and 1.96× memory\nsaving for OPT models on a single NVIDIA A100-80GB GPU, while LLM.int8() slows down the inference in\nmost cases.\nFigure 8: Inference latency (top) and memory usage (bottom) of the FasterTransformer implementation on\nNVIDIA A100-80GB GPUs. For smaller models, the latency can be signiﬁcantly reduced with SmoothQuant-\nO3 by up to 1.56x compared to FP16. For the bigger models (OPT-66B and 175B), we can achieve similar or even\nfaster inference using only half number of GPUs. Memory footprint is almost halved compared to FP16.\ninstead of 2 for OPT-66B, 4 GPUs instead of 8\nfor OPT-175B). This could greatly lower the cost\nof serving LLMs. The amount of memory needed\nwhen using SmoothQuant-O3 in FasterTransformer\nis reduced by a factor of almost 2×, as shown on\nFigure 8 (bottom).\n4.4\nAblation Study\nQuantization schemes.\nTable 6 shows the in-\nference latency of different quantization schemes\nbased on our PyTorch implementation. We can\nsee that the coarser the quantization granularity\n(from O1 to O3), the lower the latency. And static\nquantization can signiﬁcantly accelerate inference\ncompared with dynamic quantization because we\nno longer need to calculate the quantization step\nsizes at runtime. SmoothQuant is faster than FP16\nbaseline under all settings, while LLM.int8()\nis usually slower. We recommend using a coarser\nquantization scheme if the accuracy permits.\nMigration strength.\nWe need to ﬁnd a suitable\nmigration strength α (see Equation 4) to balance\nthe quantization difﬁculty of weights and activa-\ntions. We ablate the effect of different α’s on OPT-\n175B with LAMBADA in Figure 9. When α is\ntoo small (<0.4), the activations are hard to quan-\ntize; when α is too large (>0.6), the weights will be\nhard to quantize. Only when we choose α from the\nsweet spot region (0.4-0.6) can we get small quanti-\nzation errors for both weights and activations, and\nmaintain the model performance after quantization.\n5\nRelated Work\nLarge language models (LLMs).\nPre-trained\nlanguage models have achieved remarkable per-\nformance on various benchmarks by scaling up.\nGPT-3 (Brown et al., 2020b) is the ﬁrst LLM be-\n\n\n=== PDF page 9 ===\nModel\nOPT-13B\nOPT-30B\nSequence Length\n256\n512\n256\n512\nFP16\n152.6\n296.3\n343.0\n659.9\nLLM.int8()\n237.1\n371.5\n387.9\n654.9\nSmoothQuant-O1\n124.5\n243.3\n246.7\n490.7\nSmoothQuant-O2\n120.5\n235.1\n240.2\n478.3\nSmoothQuant-O3\n112.1\n223.1\n227.6\n458.4\nTable 6: GPU Latency (ms) of different quantization\nschemes. The coarser the quantization scheme (from\nper-token to per-tensor, dynamic to static, O1 to O3, de-\nﬁned in Table 3), the lower the latency. SmoothQuant\nachieves lower latency compared to FP16 under all set-\ntings, while LLM.int8() is mostly slower. The batch\nsize is 4.\nFigure 9: A suitable migration strength α (sweet spot)\nmakes both activations and weights easy to quantize. If\nthe α is too large, weights will be hard to quantize; if\ntoo small, activations will be hard to quantize.\nyond 100B parameters and achieves impressive\nfew-shot/zero-shot learning results. Later work\nlike Gopher (Rae et al., 2021), Megatron-Turing\nNLG (Smith et al., 2022), GLaM (Du et al., 2022),\nPaLM (Chowdhery et al., 2022), etc. continue to\npush the frontier of scaling, going beyond 500B\nparameters. However, as the language model gets\nlarger, serving such models for inference becomes\nexpensive and challenging. In this work, we show\nthat our proposed method can quantize the three\nlargest, openly available LLMs: OPT-175B (Zhang\net al., 2022), BLOOM-176B (Scao et al., 2022)\nand GLM-130B (Zeng et al., 2022), to reduce the\nmemory cost and accelerate inference.\nModel quantization.\nQuantization is an effec-\ntive method for reducing the model size and ac-\ncelerating inference. It proves to be effective for\nvarious convolutional neural works (CNNs) (Han\net al., 2016; Jacob et al., 2018; Nagel et al., 2019;\nWang et al., 2019; Lin et al., 2020) and transform-\ners (Shen et al., 2020; Kim et al., 2021; Liu et al.,\n2021). Weight equalization (Nagel et al., 2019) and\nchannel splitting (Zhao et al., 2019) reduce quanti-\nzation error by suppressing the outliers in weights.\nHowever, these techniques cannot address the ac-\ntivation outliers, which are the major quantization\nbottleneck for LLMs (Dettmers et al., 2022).\nQuantization of LLMs.\nZeroQuant (Yao et al.,\n2022) and nuQmm (Park et al., 2022) use a\nper-tensor and group-wise quantization scheme\nfor LLMs, which requires customized CUDA\nkernels.\nTheir largest evaluated models are\n20B and 2.7B, respectively and fail to maintain\nthe performance of larger LMs like OPT-175B.\nLLM.int8() (Dettmers et al., 2022) uses mixed\nINT8/FP16 decomposition to address the activation\noutliers. However, such implementation leads to\nlarge latency overhead, which can be even slower\nthan FP16 inference. Outlier Suppression (Wei\net al., 2022) uses the non-scaling LayerNorm and\ntoken-wise clipping to deal with the activation out-\nliers. However, it only succeeds on small language\nmodels such as BERT (Devlin et al., 2019) and\nBART (Lewis et al., 2019) and fails to maintain\nthe accuracy for LLMs (Table 5). Our algorithm\npreserves the performance of LLMs (up to 176B,\nthe largest open-source LLM we can ﬁnd) with\nan efﬁcient per-tensor, static quantization scheme\nwithout retraining, allowing us to use off-the-shelf\nINT8 GEMM to achieve high hardware efﬁciency.\n6\nConclusion\nWe propose SmoothQuant, an accurate and efﬁ-\ncient post-training quantization method to enable\nlossless 8-bit weight and activation quantization for\nLLMs with 176 billion parameters. SmoothQuant\nmigrates quantization difﬁculty from activations\nto weights by doing a mathematically equivalent\ntransformation to the model parameters, which\neffectively smooths the activation outliers while\nmaintaining low quantization error for the weights.\nSmoothQuant enables the quantization for both\nweight and activations for all GEMMs in the LLMs,\nwhich signiﬁcantly reduces the inference latency\nand memory usage compared with the mixed-\nprecision activation quantization baseline. We inte-\ngrate SmoothQuant into PyTorch and FasterTrans-\nformer, getting up to 1.56× inference acceleration\nand halving the memory footprint. SmoothQuant\ndemocratizes the application of LLMs by offering\na turnkey solution to reduce the serving cost.\n\n\n=== PDF page 10 ===\nAcknowledgements\nWe thank MIT-IBM Watson AI Lab, MIT AI Hard-\nware Program, Amazon and MIT Science Hub,\nQualcomm Innovation Fellowship, NVIDIA Aca-\ndemic Partnership Award, Microsoft Turing Aca-\ndemic Program, and NSF for supporting this re-\nsearch. In this\npaper, we propose Activation-aware Weight Quantization (AWQ), a hardware-\nfriendly approach for LLM low-bit weight-only quantization. Our method is\nbased on the observation that weights are not equally important: protecting only\n1% of salient weights can greatly reduce quantization error. We then propose\nto search for the optimal per-channel scaling that protects the salient weights by\nobserving the activation, not weights. AWQ does not rely on any backpropagation\nor reconstruction, so it can well preserve LLMs’ generalization ability on different\ndomains and modalities, without overfitting to the calibration set; it also does not\nrely on any data layout reordering, maintaining the hardware efficiency. AWQ\noutperforms existing work on various language modeling, common sense QA, and\ndomain-specific benchmarks. Thanks to better generalization, it achieves excellent\nquantization performance for instruction-tuned LMs and, for the first time, multi-\nmodal LMs. We also implement efficient tensor core kernels with reorder-free\nonline dequantization to accelerate AWQ, achieving a 1.45× speedup over GPTQ\nand is 1.85× faster than the cuBLAS FP16 implementation. Our method provides\na turn-key solution to compress LLMs to 3/4 bits for efficient deployment.\n1\nIntroduction\nLarge language models (LLMs) based on transformers [48] have shown excellent performance on\nvarious benchmarks [5, 58, 47, 43]. However, the large model size leads to the high serving costs.\nFor example, GPT-3 has 175B parameters, which is 350GB in FP16, while the latest H100 GPU only\nhas 96GB memory, let alone edge devices.\nLow-bit weight quantization for LLMs can save memory but is hard. Quantization-aware training\n(QAT) is not practical due to the high training cost, while post-training quantization (PTQ) suffers\nfrom large accuracy degradation under a low-bit setting. The closest work is GPTQ [17], which uses\nsecond-order information to perform error compensation. But it relies on a reordering technique to\nwork for some models (e.g., OPT-66B and LLaMA-7B), which is hardware inefficient (about 2×\nslower, Figure 6). It may also over-fit the calibration set during reconstruction, distorting the learned\nfeatures on out-of-distribution domains (Figure 7), which could be a problem for multi-modal models.\nIn this paper, we propose Activation-aware Weight Quantization (AWQ), a hardware-friendly low-bit\nweight-only quantization method for LLMs. Our method is based on the observation that weights\nare not equally important for LLMs’ performance. There is a small fraction (0.1%-1%) of salient\nweights; skipping the quantization of these salient weights will significantly reduce the quantization\nloss (Table 1). To find the salient weight channels, the insight is that we should refer to the activation\n∗indicates equal contributions.\nPreprint. To avoid mixed precision, we follow the activation-awareness principle and design\na per-channel scaling method to search for the optimal scaling factors that minimize the quantization\nerror, and quantize all the weights.\nAWQ does not rely on any backpropagation or reconstruction, so it can well preserve LLMs’ gen-\neralization ability on various domains and modalities without overfitting to the calibration set. It\ndoes not rely on any data layout reordering and preservers memory regularity, which is 2× faster\nthan reordering based methods. We also implement efficient GPU kernels from scratch to support\nAWQ. We employ reorder-free online dequantization to efficiently convert low-bit weights to FP16\nand effectively map to high-throughput tensor cores rather than cuda cores.\nExperiments show that AWQ outperforms existing work on various tasks (language modeling, com-\nmon sense QA, and domain-specific benchmarks) for different model families (e.g., LLaMA [47],\nOPT [58]) and model sizes. Thanks to better generalization, it also achieves good quantization\nperformance for instruction-tuned LMs (e.g., Vicuna) and, for the first time, multi-modal LMs (Open-\nFlamingo [2]). Thanks to our efficient kernels, AWQ achieves 1.45× and 2× speedup over GPTQ\nand GPTQ with reordering on A100. It is also 1.85× faster than an FP16 cuBLAS implementation.\nAWQ makes multi-modal LM more efficient without hurting the generalization capability.\n2\nActivation-aware Weight Quantization (AWQ)\nPreliminaries.\nQuantization maps a floating-point number into lower-bit integers. It is an effective\nmethod to reduce the model size and inference costs of LLMs [12, 17, 55, 54]. There are typically\ntwo quantization settings for LLM quantization: 1. W8A8 quantization [12, 54], which maps both\nactivation and weights into INT8; 2. Low-bit weight-only quantization [17, 39], which quantizes\nweights into low-bit integers (typically ≤4 bits). The decoding stage dominates LLMs’ total run-\ntime [17, 32], which is highly memory-bounded with a single batch size. Given that the memory is\ndominated by weights, we focus on weight-only quantization. We study group-wise quantization\n(with group size 128 [17]) since it helps to improve the accuracy-cost trade-off [13] at little overhead.\nIn this section, we first propose a method to improve quantized performance without training/regres-\nsion by protecting more \"important\" weights. And then develop a data-driven method to search for\nthe optimal scaling that reduces quantization errors (Figure 1).\n2.1\nImproving LLM Quantization by Preserving 1% Salient Weights\nWe observe that the weights of LLMs are not equally important: there is a small fraction of salient\nweights that are much more important for LLMs’ performance compared to others. Skipping\nthe quantization of these salient weights can help bridge the performance degradation due to the\nquantization loss without any training or regression (Figure 1(b)). To verify the idea, we benchmark\nthe performance of quantized LLMs when skipping part of the weight channels in Table 1. We\nmeasured the performance of INT3 quantized models while keeping some ratios of weight channels\nin FP16. A widely used method to determine the importance of weights is to look at its magnitude or\nL2-norm [22, 16]. But we find skipping the weight channels with large norm (i.e., FP16% (based\non W)) does not significantly improve the quantized performance, leading to a similar marginal\n2\n\n\n=== PDF page 3 ===\nPPL ↓\nFP16\nRTN\nFP16% (based on act.)\nFP16% (based on W)\nFP16% (random)\n(w3-g128)\n0.1%\n1%\n3%\n0.1%\n1%\n3%\n0.1%\n1%\n3%\nOPT-1.3B\n16.41\n206.5\n28.00\n18.51\n18.30\n187.1\n173.1\n165.5\n211.2\n201.4\n88.44\nOPT-6.7B\n12.29\n43.16\n13.14\n13.02\n12.97\n43.51\n38.59\n39.78\n42.73\n37.83\n46.49\nOPT-13B\n11.50\n45.37\n12.14\n12.04\n12.00\n46.21\n48.07\n54.38\n45.95\n44.47\n40.01\nTable 1. Keeping a small fraction of weights (0.1%-1%) in FP16 significantly improves the performance of the\nquantized models over round-to-nearest (RTN). It is only effective when we select the important weights in FP16\nby looking at activation distribution inst\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 12000:44000]\nio for each input channel that achieves the goal.\nSearching to scale. We automatically search for an optimal (per input channel) scaling factor that\nminimizes the output difference after quantization for a certain layer. Formally, we want to optimize\nthe following objective:\ns∗= arg min\ns\nL(s),\nL(s) = ∥Q(W · s)(s−1 · X) −WX∥\n(1)\n*There is a small chance that multiple outliers appear in the same quantization group. But it would be rare\nwhen using a small outlier ratio like 0.1%, so we will ignore it for the study.\n3\n\n\n=== PDF page 4 ===\nHere Q means the weight quantization function (e.g., INT3/INT4 quantization with group size 128),\nW is the original weights in FP16, and X is the input features cached from a small calibration set (we\ntake a small calibration set from he pre-training dataset in order not to overfit to a specific task). s is a\nper-(input) channel scaling factor; for s−1 · X, it can usually be fused into the previous operator [52,\n54]. Since the quantization function is not differentiable, we are not able to directly optimize\nthe problem with vanilla backpropagation. There are some techniques relying on approximated\ngradients [3, 15], which we found still converges poorly and has the potential risk of over-fitting.\nWe design the search space for the optimal scale by analyzing the factors that will affect the choice\nof scaling factor. Intuitively, the optimal scale should be related to: 1. Activation magnitude: as\nmentioned above, we are relying on input activation magnitude to pick out the salient weight channels.\nTherefore, we should consider the magnitude of X to protect the salient weight channels. We compute\nthe average activation magnitude sX = meanc_out|X| as the importance factor. 2. Weight magnitude:\nto minimize the quantization loss of non-salient weights, we should flatten their distribution so that it\nis easier for quantization [54]. A reasonable choice is to divide the weight channels by their average\nmagnitude sW = meanc_out| ˆ\nW| [54], where ˆ\nW refers to W after normalization within each group.\nWe determine the final scaling factors by a function considering both of the factors s = f(sX, sW).\nFor simplicity, we multiply both scales and solve:\ns = f(sX, sW) = sX\nα · sW\n−β,\nα∗, β∗= arg min\nα,β\nL(sX\nα · sW\n−β)\n(2)\nα and β are hyper-parameters to control the strength of each component. We can pick the optimal\nα and β with a simple grid search over the interval [0, 1]. As shown in Table 2, scaling based on\nsX significantly outperforms sW, demonstrating the importance of activation-awareness. Further\nintroducing sW only brings a marginal improvement, which again shows activation-awareness is most\ncrucial. Furthermore, we find adjusting the clipping range by searching a shrinking ratio (denoted\nas “+clip”) can sometimes further improve the quantized performance. Adjusting the clipping range\nleads to a nudged scaling factor [15] which may help better protect the salient weights. We combine\nboth scaling and clipping to form AWQ.\nExplaining GPTQ reordering. Our principle of activation-awareness can also explain why we\nneed reordering to make GPTQ work on some models (e.g., LLaMA-7B and OPT-66B, please\nrefer to the --act-order option in GPTQ’s repo†). Different weight channels have different\nimportance; updating the salient channels to compensate for the non-salient ones will likely destroy\nthe performance. Reordering prevents it by quantizing important channels first. However, it will lead\nto bad hardware efficiency due to irregular memory access (Figure 2), while our scaling method does\nnot suffer from the issue.\nAdvantages. Furthermore, our method does not rely on any regression [17] or backpropagation,\nwhich is required by many quantization-aware training methods. It has minimal reliance on the\ncalibration set since we only measure the average magnitude per channel, thus preventing over-fitting\n(Figure 7). Therefore, our method requires fewer data for the quantization process and can preserve\nLLMs’ knowledge outside of the calibration set’s distribution. See Section 3.4 for more details.\n2.3\nCo-design Efficient Quantized GPU Kernels\nThe hardware-friendly nature of AWQ facilitates the development of highly efficient\nGPU kernels, effectively translating theoretical memory savings into measured speedups.\ncta \n#0\nRandom \naccess\ns0,0\ns2,0\ns1,0\ns0,0\ns1,0\ns2,0\ns0,1\ns0,1\ns2,1\ns2,1\ns1,1\ns1,1\ns0,2 s0,3\ns2,2 s2,3\ns1,2 s1,3\ns0,2 s0,3\ns1,2 s1,3\ns2,2 s2,3\ns0,0\ns0,0\ns1,0\ns1,0\ns2,0\ns2,0\ns0,1\ns1,1\ns0,1\ns2,1\ns2,1\ns1,1\ns0,2 s0,3\ns0,2 s0,3\ns1,2 s1,3\ns1,2 s1,3\ns2,2 s2,3\ns2,2 s2,3\nContig. \naccess\ncta \n#2\nFigure 2. Reordering leads\nto inefficient random mem-\nory access.\nUnlike GPTQ [17] which formulates linear layers as matrix-vector (MV)\nproducts, we instead model these layers as matrix-matrix (MM) multi-\nplications. MV can only be executed on slow CUDA cores while MM\ncan be executed on the 16× faster tensor cores on A100 and H100. Our\nformulation also minimizes structural hazards compared to [17] since\nMM and other instructions such as dequantization and memory access\nare executed on separate function units. We also outperform a recent\nTriton [46] implementation [40] for GPTQ by 2.4× since it relies on a\nhigh-level language and forgoes opportunities for low-level optimizations.\n†https://github.com/IST-DASLab/gptq\n4\n\n\n=== PDF page 5 ===\nLLaMA-7B\nMMLU (5-shot) ↑\nCommon Sense QA (0-shot) ↑\nHums.\nSTEM\nSocial\nOther\nAvg.\nPIQA\nHella.\nWino.\nARC-e\nAvg.\nFP16 -\n39.17% 32.32% 42.72% 42.56% 38.41% 78.35% 56.44% 67.09% 67.30% 67.30%\nINT3\ng128\nRTN\n31.37% 31.10% 36.04% 36.49% 33.43% 75.84% 53.10% 63.22% 66.04% 64.55%\nGPTQ\n29.29% 29.04% 33.03% 31.65% 30.53% 70.89% 46.77% 60.93% 60.06% 59.66%\nGPTQ-R 33.98% 30.71% 37.78% 36.49% 34.26% 77.31% 53.81% 67.56% 63.72% 65.60%\nAWQ\n35.15% 31.61% 39.27% 37.75% 35.43% 76.66% 53.63% 66.14% 65.70% 65.53%\nINT4\ng128\nRTN\n36.15% 33.03% 41.41% 41.21% 37.37% 77.86% 55.81% 65.59% 66.25% 66.38%\nGPTQ\n35.55% 30.95% 39.29% 38.12% 35.39% 77.20% 53.98% 65.67% 61.62% 64.62%\nGPTQ-R 37.28% 31.36% 40.23% 40.77% 36.72% 78.45% 56.00% 66.85% 66.88% 67.05%\nAWQ\n38.32% 32.00% 41.38% 42.07% 37.71% 78.07% 55.76% 65.82% 66.84% 66.62%\nTable 3. AWQ consistently outperforms RTN and GPTQ for LLaMA-7B under 3/4-bit quantization with a group\nsize of 128. Note that GPTQ needs the hardware-inefficient reordering trick to work on the 7B model, denoted\nas GPTQ-R, which is significantly slower (Figure 6), so we exclude it for performance comparison.\nWeight-only quantization methods require online weight dequantization. In Figure 2, we assume\nthat the weights are stored in row major (i.e. IC×OC). Each OC has different scales and zero points\nand every two ICs within each OC share dequantization parameters (i.e. g = 2). For GPTQ with\nreordering (right), since these g = 2 ICs are not continuous, irregular DRAM accesses are required\nto fetch scaling factors and zero points when dequantizing each weight. However, for AWQ (left), all\nmemory accesses are contiguous, resulting in a 2× end-to-end speedup for LLaMA models.\n3\nExperiments\n3.1\nSettings\nQuantization.\nWe focus on weight-only grouped quantization in this work. As shown in previous\nwork [13, 17], grouped quantization is always helpful for improving performance/model size trade-\noff. We used a group size of 128 throughout the work, except otherwise specified. We focus on\nINT4/INT3 quantization since they are able to mostly preserve the LLMs’ performance [13]. For\nAWQ, we used a small calibration set from the Pile [18] dataset in order not to overfit to a specific\ndownstream domain. We used a grid size of 20 to search for the optimal α and β in Equation 2.\nModels.\nWe benchmarked our method on LLaMA [47] and OPT [58] families. There are other\nopen LLMs like BLOOM [43], but they are generally worse in quality, so we do not include them in\nour study. We further benchmark an instruction-tuned model Vicuna [8] and visual language models\nOpenFlamingo-9B [2] and LLaVA-13B [31] to demonstrate the generability of our method.\nEvaluations.\nFollowing previous literature [12, 54, 17, 13, 55], we profiled the quantized models\non language modeling tasks (WikiText-2 [33]) and common sense QA benchmarks (PIQA [4],\nHellaSwag [56], WinoGrande [41], ARC [11]; we do not use LAMBADA [38] due to a known bug\nfor LLaMA evaluation). We notice that these benchmarks do not reflect LLMs’ domain-specific\nknowledge. Therefore, we further benchmark the models on MMLU [23], which consists of 57 tasks\ncovering STEM, humanities, social science, etc. It also helps to evaluate the in-context learning\nability under a few-shot setting. We used lm-eval-harness [19] to carry out all the evaluations.\nBaselines.\nOur primary baseline is vanilla round-to-nearest quantization (RTN). It is actually quite\nstrong when using a small group size like 128 [17, 13]. We also compare with a state-of-the-art method\nGPTQ [17] for LLM weight quantization. For GPTQ, we also compare with an updated version that\nuses a “reorder” trick (denoted as GPTQ-Reorder or GPTQ-R), which improves the performance\nbut leads to worse decoding efficiency. Other techniques like ZeroQuant [55], AdaRound [34], and\nBRECQ [28] rely on backpropagation to update the quantized weights, which may not easily scale\nup to large model sizes; they also do not outperform GPTQ [17], thus not included for study.\n3.2\nAccuracy Evaluation\nResults on LLaMA models.\nWe focus our study on LLaMA models due to their superior per-\nformance compared to other open-source LLMs [58, 43]; it is also the foundation of many popular\nopen-source models [45, 8]. AWQ improves over round-to-nearest quantization (RTN) for all model sizes and different bit-precisions.\nIt achieves better WikiText-2 perplexity compared to GPTQ on smaller OPT models and on-par results on larger\nones, demonstrating the generality to different model sizes and families. We found OPT performs poorly on\nMMLU and has limited in-context learning capacity (even FP16 model), so we exclude those evaluations.\n(0-shot) and MMLU benchmark with in-context learning (5-shot). We found in preliminary experi-\nments that quantization may hurt a model’s in-context learning performance, which is a fundamental\nability of LLMs [6] ; thus, MMLU (5-shot) serves as an essential benchmark in our study. We provide\nthe INT3-g128/INT4-g128 results of LLaMA-7B in Table 3; for larger models (Table 4), we find the\n4-bit RTN accuracy is already quite good, so we only include the 3-bit results. We can see that AWQ\noutperforms round-to-nearest (RTN) and GPTQ [17] across different model scales (7B-65B), task\ntypes (common sense vs. domain-specific), and test settings (zero-shot vs. in-context learning). Note\nthat the RTN baseline with a group size of 128 is already quite strong (only a few percent accuracy\ndrop), so a 1% error reduction is considered significant. For the LLaMA-7B model, we need to\ninclude the reordering trick to make GPTQ work, which leads to hardware-inefficient memory access\nand a 2× slow down in kernel implementation (Figure 6). Therefore, we only provide the results for\nreference but exclude them for comparison (in gray).\nResults on OPT models.\nWe also provide the results on OPT models [58]. We found that OPT\nmodels generally achieve very low MMLU accuracy (may not be meaningful) and have limited in-\ncontext learning capacity (please see Table 9 in supplementary). Therefore, we focus on WikiText-2\nperplexity evaluation following [17]. As shown in Table 5, AWQ improves both INT3 and INT4\ngrouped quantization. For smaller models (<13B), our method can outperform the competitive GPTQ\nresults despite its simplicity; while for larger models, we are able to obtain on-par performance. This\ndemonstrates the generality to different model families and model sizes.\nQuantization of instruction-tuned models.\nInstruction tuning can significantly improve the\nmodels’ performance and usability [50, 42, 37, 10]. It has become an essential procedure before\nmodel deployment. We further benchmark our method’s performance on a popular instruction-\ntuned model Vicuna [8] in Figure 3. We used the GPT-4 score to evaluate the quantized models’\nperformance against the FP16 counterpart on 80 sample questions [8]. We compare the responses\nwith both orders (quantized-FP16, FP16-quantized) to get rid of the ordering effect (we found GPT-4\ntends to increase the rating of the first input), leading to 160 trials. AWQ consistently improves\nthe INT3-g128 quantized Vicuna models over RTN and GPTQ under both scales (7B and 13B),\ndemonstrating the generability to instruction-tuned models.\n6\n\n\n=== PDF page 7 ===\n0\n40\n80\n120\n160\n96\n148\n119\n22\n8\n13\n42\n4\n28\nQuantized Win\nTie\nQuantized Lost\n0\n40\n80\n120\n160\n78\n87\n100\n26\n26\n21\n56\n47\n39\nINT3/g128\nRTN \nGPTQ \nAWQ\n(a) Vicuna-7B\n(b) Vicuna-13B\nFigure 3. AWQ reduces the quantization degradation (32-shot)\nfrom 4.57 to 1.17 under INT4-g128, providing 4× model size reduction with negligible performance loss.\nQuantization of multi-modal language models.\nLarge multi-modal models (LMMs) or visual\nlanguage models (VLMs) are LLMs augmented with vision inputs [1, 27, 26, 14, 57, 31]. Such\nmodels are able to perform text generation conditioned on image/video inputs. Since our method\ndoes not have the overfitting issue to the calibration set, it can be directly applied to VLMs to provide\naccurate and efficient quantization. We perform experiments with the OpenFlamingo-9B model [2]\n(an open-source reproduction of [1]) on COCO captioning [7] dataset (Table 6). We measured the\naverage performance of 5k samples under different few-shot settings. We only quantize the language\npart of the model since it dominates the model size. AWQ outperforms existing methods under\nzero-shot and various few-shot settings, demonstrating the generability to different modalities and\nin-context learning workloads. It reduces the quantization degradation (32-shot) from 4.57 to 1.17\nunder INT4-g128, providing 4× model size reduction with negligible performance loss. We further\nprovide some qualitative captioning results in Figure 4 to show our advantage over RTN. Our method\nprovides a push-the-button solution for LMM/VLM quantization. It is the first study of VLM low-bit\nquantization to the best of our knowledge.\nVisual reasoning results.\nWe further provide some qualitative visual reasoning examples of the\nLLaVA-13B [31] model in Figure 5. AWQ improves the responses compared to the round-to-nearest\n(RTN) baseline for INT4-g128 quantization, leading to more reasonable answers. In this first example,\nthe AWQ model can understand the meme as it resembles the Earth when looking from space, while\nRTN produces wrong descriptions (marked in red). In the second example, AWQ correctly answers\nthe question (the artist of the painting), while RTN does not provide any information about the\nartist. In the last example, RTN falsely points out a bird in the picture, while AWQ provides more\ninformation by noticing the image is taken in a mountain area. AWQ improves the visual reasoning\nability of VLMs by reducing factual errors in the responses; RTN is not good enough even for 4 bits.\nExtreme low-bit quantization.\nWe further quantize LLM to INT2 to accommodate limited device\nmemory (Table 7). RTN completely fails, and AWQ brings significant perplexity improvement on\ntop of GPTQ, though there is still a performance gap compared to FP16. Our method is orthogonal\nto GPTQ. We can combine our method with GPTQ to further improve the INT2 quantization\nperformance, making it a more practical setting.\n3.3\nEfficiency Evaluation\nComparison with GPTQ kernels.\nIn Figure 6(a), we compare the end-to-end efficiency of LLaMA\nmodels quantized using AWQ and GPTQ. By following these precautions, you can ensure a safe and enjoyable visit to this picturesque \nlocation.\nFigure 5. Visual reasoning examples from LLaVA-13B model [31]. AWQ improves over the round-to-nearest\n(RTN) baseline, providing more reasonable answers. We color the text to show the correct or wrong responses.\nsupport 4-bit weights and reordering, we present the latency of 3-bit GPTQ without reordering.\nRemarkably, despite utilizing an additional bit per weight, AWQ achieves an average speedup of\n1.45×, and a maximum speedup of 1.7× over GPTQ. We attribute this efficiency improvement to our\nformulation of linear layers in LLMs as matrix-matrix instead of matrix-vector product. As such, we\ncan take advantage of high-throughput tensor cores on the A100 GPU and avoid resource contention\nbetween computation and data preparation (e.g. dequantization and memory access) instructions.\nOverhead of reordering and compilers.\nFigure 6(b) demonstrates the significance of eliminating\nreordering during dequantization. To support GPTQ-R, we made only one modification to the AWQ\nkernels: introducing indirect addressing when loading zero points and scales in weight dequantization.\nIt turns out that AWQ with reorder-free kernels are 1.4-2.4× faster than GPTQ-R. This clearly\nindicates that the irregular memory access patterns in GPTQ-R kernels are not hardware-friendly and\nhave a significant negative impact on performance. Furthermore, we note that a direct CUDA-level\nimplementation is essential to achieve consistent performance gains over cuBLAS and GPTQ. We\nbenchmark AWQ kernels against an open-source implementation [40] based on the Triton [46]\ncompiler and achieved 1.8-3.1× speedup. The Triton kernels become 1.6× slower than the FP16\ncuBLAS kernels in LLaMA-30B, while AWQ kernels are still 1.8× faster. This further emphasizes\nthe limitations of compilers in identifying and exploiting low-level optimization opportunities.\n8\n\n\n=== PDF page 9 ===\nOPT / Wiki PPL↓\n1.3B\n2.7B\n6.7B\n13B\n30B\nFP16\n-\n16.41\n14.32\n12.29\n11.5\n10.67\nINT2\ng64\nRTN\n26615\n740860\n22290\n28923\n15198\nGPTQ\n50.05\n30.41\n19.04\n16.8\n12.91\nAWQ +GPTQ\n35.26\n24.02\n17.34\n14.58\n12.5\nTable 7. All\nnumbers are measured on a single 80G A100 GPU.\n3.4\nAnalysis\nBetter data-efficiency for the calibration set.\nOur method requires a smaller calibration set since\nwe do not rely on regression/backpropagation; we only measure the average activation scale from\nthe calibration set, which is data-efficient. To demonstrate the idea, we compare the perplexity of\nthe OPT-6.7B model with INT3-g128 quantization in Figure 7 (a). AWQ needs a much smaller\ncalibration to reach a good quantized performance; it can achieve better perplexity using 10× smaller\ncalibration set compared to GPTQ (16 sequences vs. 192 sequences).\nRobust to the calibration set distributions.\nOur method is less sensitive to the calibration set\ndistribution since we only measure the average activation scale from the calibration set, which is more\ngeneralizable across different dataset distributions. We further benchmarked the effect of the different\ncalibration set distributions in Figure 7(b). We took two subsets from the Pile dataset [18]: PubMed\nAbstracts and Enron Emails [25]. We use each of the subsets as the calibration set and evaluate\nthe quantized model on both sets (the calibration and evaluation sets are split with no overlapping;\nwe used 1k samples for evaluation). Overall, using the same calibration and evaluation distribution\nworks the best (PubMed-PubMed, Enron-Enron). But when using a different calibration distribution\n(PubMed-Enron, Enron-PubMed), AWQ only increases the perplexity by 0.5-0.6, while GPTQ has\n2.3-4.9 worse perplexity. This demonstrates the robustness of AWQ to the calibration set distribution.\nDifferentiation with SmoothQuant.\nAt first glance, the derived formula (Equation 2) is related\nto SmoothQuant [54], where it balances activation and weight smoothness (actually it is different:\nwe only need sX, as the weight distribution has a minimal contribution to the performance, see\nTable 2). However, our method is fundamentally different from SmoothQuant. Firstly, the intuition\nand mechanism are different. SmoothQuant balances the smoothness between weight and activation\nsince both of them are quantized using the same INT8 quantizer. It should only consider weight\ndistributions in our setting (only weights are quantized), while we found that activation distribution\nis essential for the weight quantization but not the weight distribution (Table 2). Secondly, we are\nfocusing on low-bit weight-only quantization, where the SmoothQuant formula cannot achieve decent\nperformance. Take LLaMA-7B INT3-g128 quantization as an example: the default SmoothQuant\n(α=0.5) achieves 11.55 WikiText-2 perplexity. Using α = 0 (migrate quantization difficulty from\nweight to activation) actually makes the perplexity worse at 12.00, which contradicts the story of\nSmoothQuant and is the same as RTN baseline at 12.10, while our method can achieve 11.07.\n4\nRelated Work\nModel quantization methods.\nQuantization reduces the bit-precision of deep learning models [21,\n24, 35, 49, 34, 30], which helps to reduce the model size and accelerate inference. Quantization\n9\n\n\n=== PDF page 10 ===\nPerplexity\n13\n13.5\n14\n8\n16\n32\n64\n128\n192\n256\nGPTQ\nOurs\nGPTQ\nOurs\nPubMed Enron\nPubMed\nEnron\nPubMed\n32.48\n50.41\n32.56\n45.07\nEnron\n34.81\n45.52\n33.16\n44.57\nCalib\nEval\n+2.33\n+4.89\n+0.60\n+0.50\n# calibration sequences (×2048 tokens)\n(a) Our method needs a smaller calibration set\n(b) Our method is more robust to calibration set distribution\nFigure 7. Left: AWQ needs a much smaller calibration set to reach a good quantized performance. It can\nachieve better perplexity using 10× smaller calibration set compared to GPTQ. Right: Our method is more\nrobust to the calibration set distribution. Overall, using the same calibration and evaluation distribution works\nthe best (PubMed-PubMed, Enron-Enron). But when using a different calibration distribution (PubMed-Enron,\nEnron-PubMed), AWQ only increases the perplexity by 0.5-0.6, while GPTQ has 2.3-4.9 worse perplexity. All\nexperiments are done with the OPT-6.7B model under INT3-g128 quantization.\ntechniques generally fall into two categories: quantization-aware training (QAT, which relies on\nbackpropagation to update the quantized weights) [3, 20, 36, 9] and post-training quantization [24,\n35, 34] (PTQ, usually training-free). The QAT methods cannot easily scale up to large models like\nLLMs. Therefore, people usually use PTQ methods to quantize LLMs.\nQuantization of LLMs.\nPeople study two settings for LLM quantization: (1) W8A8 quantization,\nwhere both activation and weights are quantized to INT8 [12, 54, 55, 53, 51]; (2) Low-bit weight-only\nquantization (e.g., W4A16), where only weights are quantized into low-bit integers [17, 13, 44, 39].\nWe focus on the second setting in this work since it not only reduces the hardware barrier (requiring a\nsmaller memory size) but also speeds up the token generation (remedies memory-bound workload).\nApart from the vanilla round-to-nearest baseline (RTN), GPTQ [17] is the closest to our work.\nHowever, the reconstruction process of GPTQ leads to an over-fitting issue to the calibration set and\nmay not preserve the generalist abilities of LLMs for other modalities and domains. It also requires\na reordering trick to work for some models (e.g., LLaMA-7B [47] and OPT-66B [58]), leading to\nhardware-inefficient irregular memory access and slowing down the inference.\nSystem support for low-bit quantized LLMs.\nLow-bit quantized LLMs have been a popular\nsetting to reduce inference costs. There are some system supports to achieve a practical speed-up.\nGPTQ [17] provides INT3 kernels for OPT models and GPTQ-for-LLaMA extends kernel sup-\nport for INT4 reordered quantization with the help of the Triton compiler [46]. FlexGen [44] and\nllama.cpp‡ perform group-wise INT4 quantization to reduce I/O costs and offloading. Faster-\nTransformer§ implements FP16×INT4 GEMM for weight-only per-tensor quantization but does not\nsupport group quantization. LUT-GEMM [39] performs bitwise computation on GPU CUDA cores\nwith the help of lookup tables. Our AWQ kernels are executed on tensor cores, suitable for both\ncontext and generation phases in LLM inference, and do not require hardware-inefficient reordering.\nTherefore, our kernels are 1.45× faster than the best competitor when running LLaMA models.\n5\nConclusion\nIn this work, we propose Activation-aware Weight Quantization (AWQ), a simple yet effective\nmethod for low-bit weight-only LLM compression. AWQ is based on the observation that weights\nare not equally important in LLMs and performs per-channel scaling to reduce the quantization loss\nof salient weights. AWQ does not over-fit the calibration set and preserves the generalist abilities\nof LLMs in various domains and modalities. It outperforms existing work on language modeling\nand QA benchmarks and can be applicable to instruction-tuned LMs and multi-\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 52000:60000]\nent\nLLMs in the Inference Time. In this paper, we propose a general technique to enable accurate and efficient low-bit\nweight-only quantization of large language models (LLMs). It makes LLMs more efficient and accessible and\nthus may inherit the impacts of LLMs. On the positive side, quantization helps to democratize LLMs, which\nhelps to benefit more people (especially those with lower income). It reduces the costs and hardware barrier\nof deploying LLMs and facilitates edge inference of these models, addressing the data privacy issue (since we\nno longer need to send data to the cloud). On the negative side, LLMs may be exploited by malicious users to\nproduce misinformation and manipulation. Quantization can not prevent such negative effects but it does not\nmake it worse.\nLimitations. In this paper, we follow previous work [12, 17, 54, 55, 13] to mostly benchmark the quantized\nmodels on standard accuracy metrics like perplexity and accuracy. However, besides accuracy, there are other\nimportant metrics for LLM benchmark like robustness, fairness, bias, toxicity, helpfulness, calibration, etc. [29].\nWe think it would be helpful to perform a more holistic evaluation of quantized LLMs covering these aspects,\nwhich we leave to future work. Furthermore, we only study low-bit integer quantization of LLMs due to easier\ndata type casting on hardware. There might be a further improvement from changing data types (e.g., FP4 [13]),\nwhich we do not include in the study.\nB\nAmount of Computation\nWe study the post-training quantization (PTQ) of LLMs in this work. The computation requirement is generally\nmodest since we do not rely on any backpropagation. We used one NVIDIA A100 GPU for smaller models\n(<40B parameters) and 2-4 A100 GPUs for larger models due to memory limits.\nThe quantization process is generally fast, requiring a few GPU hours (rang", "excerpt_ranges": [[0, 8896], [12000, 44000], [52000, 60000]], "range_unit": "zero-based half-open characters in extracted text", "extracted_characters": 63304, "selected_characters": 48896, "extraction": "UTF-8 text", "selection_reason": "Ranked 4000-character windows by current entry title/tags/questions and algorithm/mechanism keywords; ties retain source order."}, {"path": "references/S-FLASHINFERPAPER/fulltext.txt", "sha256": "7440b4da00b96c8e0445ea408883d853adcef30cd63efa7e20071b60a12712ba", "reading_scope": "excerpt", "category": "source_original", "content": "[EXCERPT extracted-character range 0:34363]\n=== PDF page 1 ===\nFLASHINFER: EFFICIENT AND CUSTOMIZABLE ATTENTION ENGINE FOR\nLLM INFERENCE SERVING\nZihao Ye * 1 2 Lequn Chen 3 Ruihang Lai 4 Wuwei Lin 2 Yineng Zhang 5 Stephanie Wang 1 Tianqi Chen 4 2\nBaris Kasikci 1 Vinod Grover 2 Arvind Krishnamurthy 1 Luis Ceze 1 2\nABSTRACT\nTransformers, driven by attention mechanisms, form the foundation of large language models (LLMs). As these\nmodels scale up, efficient GPU attention kernels become essential for high-throughput and low-latency inference.\nDiverse LLM applications demand flexible and high-performance attention solutions. We present FlashInfer: a\ncustomizable and efficient attention engine for LLM serving. FlashInfer tackles KV-cache storage heterogeneity\nusing block-sparse format and composable formats to optimize memory access and reduce redundancy. It also\noffers a customizable attention template, enabling adaptation to various settings through Just-In-Time (JIT)\ncompilation. Additionally, FlashInfer’s load-balanced scheduling algorithm adjusts to dynamism of user requests\nwhile maintaining compatibility with CUDAGraph which requires static configuration. FlashInfer have been\nintegrated into leading LLM serving frameworks like SGLang, vLLM and MLC-Engine. Comprehensive kernel-\nlevel and end-to-end evaluations demonstrate FlashInfer’s ability to significantly boost kernel performance across\ndiverse inference scenarios: compared to state-of-the-art LLM serving solutions, FlashInfer achieve 29-69%\ninter-token-latency reduction compared to compiler backends for LLM serving benchmark, 28-30% latency\nreduction for long-context inference, and 13-17% speedup for LLM serving with parallel generation.\n1\nINTRODUCTION\nThe Transformer architecture has become the primary back-\nbone for large language models (LLMs), prominently featur-\ning attention mechanism (Vaswani et al., 2017) as its most\nsalient component. As LLMs rapidly evolve and find appli-\ncations in diverse fields, the demand for efficient GPU atten-\ntion kernels grows, with the goal of enabling scalable and\nresponsive model inference. At the heart of LLM inference\nlies the attention computation, which plays a crucial role in\nprocessing historical context and generating outputs based\non query vectors. In LLM serving, the attention mechanism\nreads from the KV cache, which stores historical context,\nand computes outputs based on the current query. The effi-\nciency of this attention operator is paramount to the overall\nperformance of an LLM inference systems. However, cre-\nating high-performance attention kernels tailored for LLM\nserving introduces challenges not typically encountered in\ntraditional training environments.\nTwo major challenges arise when building efficient attention\n*Part of the work was done while Zihao Ye is interning at\nNVIDIA. 1Paul G. Allen School of Computer Science & Engineer-\ning, University of Washington 2NVIDIA 3Perplexity AI 4Carnegie\nMellon University 5Independent Researcher. Correspondence to:\nZihao Ye <zhye@cs.washington.edu>.\nsupport for LLM systems:\nLLM applications exhibit diverse workload patterns and\ninput dynamics. LLM serving involves various attention\ncomputation patterns, from prefill computation for context\nprocessing to batched decoding during serving (Yu et al.,\n2022). As multiple requests are processed, opportunities\nfor prefix-reuse emerge, and the introduction of tree de-\ncoding in speculative scenarios creates additional attention\npatterns (Cai et al., 2024; Miao et al., 2024; Chen et al.,\n2024). Moreover, query lengths and KV caches vary within\nbatches and over time, naive implementation might suffer\nload-imbalance issue, optimal scheduling requiring kernel\nto adapt dynamically for optimal performance.\nModern hardware implementations necessitate the cus-\ntomization of attention operators. On the memory side, ef-\nficient storage formats, such as paged attention (Kwon et al.,\n2023) and radix trees (Zheng et al., 2023b), are critical for\nmanaging the growing KV cache sizes and diverse storage\npatterns. On the compute side, crafting hardware-specific\npipelines and templates is indispensable to fully exploit the\nperformance potential of each GPU architecture (Dao, 2023;\nShah et al., 2024). Furthermore, the design must accommo-\ndate the increasing variety of attention mechanisms in mod-\nern LLMs, such as grouped attention heads (Ainslie et al.,\n2023; Shazeer, 2019), specialized masks (Beltagy et al.,\n2020), and customized attention score computations (Riv-\narXiv:2501.01005v1  [cs.DC]  2 Jan 2025\n\n\n=== PDF page 2 ===\nFlashInfer: Efficient and Customizable Attention Engine for LLM Inference Serving\nUnified KV-Cache Format\n(section 3.1)\nDynamic-aware Compiler & Runtime\n(section 3.2 & 3.3)\nPage Table\nRadix Tree\nSparse mask\nBlock-Sparse\nMatrix\nCompiler\nAttention Variant\nSpecification\nJIT\nCompiler\nKV-Cache layout\nspecification\nRuntime\nScheduler\nSequence length\ninformation\nLoad-\nbalancing\nScheduler\nAttention\nKernel\nQuery\nKV-\nCache\nOutput\nWorkspace Buffer\nTask Information\nFigure 1. Overview of the FlashInfer system design: Attention variant specifications, task information and KV-cache layout specifics are\nprovided at compile time for JIT compilation, while sequence length information is input at runtime for dynamic scheduling.\nière et al., 2024; xAI, 2023; Ramapuram et al., 2024), ne-\ncessitating flexible and scalable implementation strategies.\nThe combined complexity of workload diversity and hard-\nware heterogeneity complicates the development of a com-\nprehensive attention solution. Currently, each system imple-\nments a specialized attention solution based on a subset of\nthese characteristics, leading to high maintenance overhead\nand potential inefficiencies. To address these challenges, we\nintroduce FlashInfer1, a code-generation based attention en-\ngine designed to accelerate attention computation in LLMs.\nOur approach incorporates several key designs:\nFlashInfer utilizes a block-sparse format to tackle KV-\nCache storage heterogeneity. This format serves as a uni-\nfied data structure for various KV-Cache configurations,\nwith adjustable block sizes allowing fine-grained sparsity,\nsuch as vector-level sparsity (Chen et al., 2021; Li et al.,\n2022). This approach unifies diverse KV-Cache patterns\nand enhances memory access efficiency.\nA customizable attention template supports different\nattention variants in FlashInfer. FlashInfer provides a\ncustomizable programming interface for users to implement\ntheir attention variants. FlashInfer uses Just-In-Time (JIT)\ncompilation to translate these variants into highly optimized\nblock-sparse implementations, ensuring rapid adaptation to\nvarying attention configurations.\nFlashInfer employs a dynamic load-balanced schedul-\ning framework to handle input dynamism effectively.\nIt separates compile-time tile size selection from runtime\nscheduling, offering lightweight APIs that adaptively man-\nage scheduling with changing KV-Cache lengths during\n1Project page: https://flashinfer.ai/\ninference, while maintaining compatibility with CUDA-\nGraph’s requirement for constant configurations (Gray,\n2019; Nguyen et al., 2021).\nFigure 1 depicts our system design. We evaluated Flash-\nInfer’ performance across standard LLM serving environ-\nments and innovative scenarios, including prefix sharing and\nspeculative decoding. FlashInfer have been integrated with\nmainstream LLM serving engines, including vLLM (Kwon\net al., 2023), MLC-Engine (MLC Community, 2024; Lai\net al., 2023), and SGLang (Zheng et al., 2023b), we assessed\nits impact on end-to-end latency and throughput improve-\nments, showing significant enhancements on standard LLM\nserving benchmarks and novel applications such as long-\ncontext inference and parallel generation.\nOur contributions include:\n• Introduction of flexible block-sparse and composable\nformats addressing KV-Cache storage heterogeneity\nfor efficient memory management and access.\n• Development of a customizable attention template ac-\ncommodating diverse attention variants, ensuring high-\nperformance execution via JIT compilation.\n• Design of a dynamic scheduling framework manag-\ning input dynamism while remaining compatible with\nCUDAGraph, maximizing hardware utilization.\n• Comprehensive evaluation demonstrating substantial\nimprovements in kernel and end-to-end performance.\n2\nBACKGROUND\n2.1\nFlashAttention\nFlashAttention (Dao et al., 2022) is an efficient algorithm\nfor computing exact attention with reduced memory usage.\n\n\n=== PDF page 3 ===\nFlashInfer: Efficient and Customizable Attention Engine for LLM Inference Serving\nDuring the forward pass, it employs the online-softmax\ntrick (Milakov & Gimelshein, 2018), updating attention out-\nputs on-the-fly using a constant amount of on-chip memory,\nthus avoiding materializing the attention matrix in GPU\nglobal memory. FlashAttention2&3 (Dao, 2023; Shah et al.,\n2024) improve performance by optimizing loop ordering\nand pipeline design for Ampere and Hopper GPUs. FlashIn-\nfer builds upon these advancements.\nThe operational intensity of FlashAttention is given by\nO\n\u0010\n1\n1/lqo+1/lkv\n\u0011\n, where lqo and lkv are the query and key-\nvalue cache lengths, respectively. In LLM serving, the query\nlength is either equal to (prefill) or smaller than (decode/in-\ncremental prefill) the key-value cache length, simplifying\nthe operational intensity to O(lqo). Techniques like batch-\ning (Yu et al., 2022) do not alter this operational inten-\nsity. Multi-Query Attention (MQA) (Shazeer, 2019) and\nGrouped Query Attention (GQA) (Ainslie et al., 2023) opti-\nmize the KV-Cache size by grouping queries and sharing the\nsame KV-Cache entries. The ratio of the number of queries\nto the number of KV-Cache entries is denoted as the group\nsize g = Hqo\nHkv , enhancing operational intensity to O(g · lqo).\n2.2\nAttention Composition\nBlock-Parallel Transformer (BPT) (Liu & Abbeel, 2023)\ndemonstrates that attention outputs for the same query and\ndifferent keys/values can be composed by preserving both\nthe attention outputs and their scales. Let q be a query, and\nlet I be an index set. We define the attention scale over I\nvia the log-sum-exp operation on the attention scores:\nLSE(I) = log\nX\ni∈I\nexp(q · ki)\n(1)\nwhere ki is the i-th key vector. The corresponding attention\noutput O(I) is then\nO(I) =\nX\ni∈I\nexp(q · ki)\nexp(LSE(I)) · vi\n(2)\nWe define the Attention State for I as the tuple of attention\noutput and attention scale:\n\u0014\nO(I)\nLSE(I)\n\u0015\n. Crucially, the At-\ntention State of I ∪J can be derived by composing the\nstates of I and J . Specifically, introducing an operator ⊕:\n\u0014 O(I ∪J )\nLSE(I ∪J )\n\u0015\n=\n\u0014 O(I)\nLSE(I)\n\u0015\n⊕\n\u0014 O(J )\nLSE(J )\n\u0015\n=\n\"\nexp(LSE(I))O(I)+exp(LSE(J ))O(J )\nexp(LSE(I))+exp(LSE(J ))\nlog(exp(LSE(I)) + exp(LSE(J )))\n#\nSince ⊕is associative and commutative, multiple sets\nof attention states can be composed in any order. Ring-\nAttention (Liu et al., 2023) and Flash-Decoding (Dao et al.,\n2023) utilize this property to offload partial-attention com-\nputations, thereby reducing memory usage and improving\nhardware efficiency. In FlashInfer, the Attention State is\nadopted as the canonical output of an attention operation,\nand ⊕serves as the standard reduction operator (analogous\nto summation in GEMM) on these states.\n2.3\nBlock/Vector Sparsity\nBlock Compressed Sparse Row (BSR) is a hardware-\nefficient sparse format that groups non-zero elements into\ncontiguous matrices of size (br, bc), as opposed to the ran-\ndom scattering found in unstructured sparsity. This format\noffers several advantages over the standard Compressed\nSparse Row (CSR) format. BSR improves register reuse\nefficiency (Im et al., 2004; Buluç et al., 2009) and demon-\nstrates better compatibility with hardware matrix multipli-\ncation units on GPUs and NPUs (Narang et al., 2017; Gray\net al., 2017). In addition, it provides the ability to skip\nempty blocks, reducing computational overhead. BSR’s\nefficiency is particularly evident when subcomputations are\naligned with hardware matrix multiplication instructions,\nsuch as NVIDIA’s mma instructions. Traditionally, tensor\ncore instructions operate on minimal dimensions of 16 (or\nlarger for newer GPUs), leading most block-sparse kernels\nto use block sizes that are multiples of (16, 16). However,\nthis approach is not always optimal for applications with\nfine-grained sparsity patterns (Wang et al., 2023). Many\nattention libraries restrict their block sizes to multiples of\n(128, 128) for block-sparse attention kernels.\nRecent research (Chen et al., 2021; Li et al., 2022) has\ndemonstrated that efficient utilization of the tensor core can\nbe achieved with smaller block sizes, such as (16, 1) for\nmatrix B in GEMM, or (1, 16) for matrix A (also known as\nvector-sparse). This is accomplished by first gathering rows/-\ncolumns into contiguous shared memory and then applying\ndense tensor cores to these contiguous shared-memory data.\nThis approach is particularly beneficial for applications with\nfine-grained sparsity patterns. FlashInfer builds upon these\ntechniques to support blocks with arbitrary column sizes Bc,\noffering greater flexibility and efficiency in handling diverse\nsparsity patterns.\n3\nDESIGN\nIn this section, we introduce the system design of FlashInfer.\nWe begin by presenting the data structure employed in Flash-\nInfer and demonstrate how Block-Sparse Row (BSR) acts as\na versatile abstraction for KV cache storage in attention ker-\nnels. Next, we discuss the FlashInfer compiler, which sup-\nports various attention variants, alongside a dynamic-aware\n\n\n=== PDF page 4 ===\nFlashInfer: Efficient and Customizable Attention Engine for LLM Inference Serving\nruntime scheduler that facilitates load-balanced scheduling\nof attention kernels. Finally, we describe the user-level\nAPI designed for integrating FlashInfer with existing LLM\nserving systems.\n3.1\nKV-Cache Storage\n3.1.1\nBlock-Sparse Matrix as Unified Format\nRecent advancements in KV-Cache storage, such as PageAt-\ntention (Kwon et al., 2023) and RadixAttention (Zheng\net al., 2023b), employ non-contiguous memory storage with\na minimum granularity of a block (or token) of (H, D) ten-\nsors, where H represents the number of heads and D the\nhidden dimension. These structures are optimized to mini-\nmize memory fragmentation while enhancing memory reuse\nand cache hit rates. We demonstrate that these diverse data\nstructures can be unified under a block sparse format, as\nillustrated in Figure 2.\n#KV physical page idx\nRequest 1\nRequest 2\nRequest 3\nQuery\nLogical KV-Cache Block\nPhysical KV-Cache Block\nRequest id\n1\n2\n0\n(H * D)\nBlock Sparse Matrix\n = (8, 4, 4)\nPage size (\n) = 1\nQuery tile size(\n) = 4\nFigure 2. Representation of Page Table in BSR (Br = 4, Bc = 1)\nformat. The number of column blocks in the block sparse matrix\ncorresponds to the total number of blocks allocated for the Page\nTable. Non-zero blocks represent KV-Cache pages accessed by\nqueries.\nThe conceptual equivalence between page tables and sparse\nmatrices has been previously explored in SPGrid (Setaluri\net al., 2014), which leverages Translation Lookaside Buffer\n(TLB) hardware for efficient sparse structure indexing. Be-\nyond page tables and radix trees, sparse matrices can also\neffectively represent various attention mechanisms, such\nas Tree Attentions used in speculative decoding (Cai et al.,\n2024; Miao et al., 2024; Chen et al., 2024) and importance\nmasks applied to KV-Cache (Tang et al., 2024).\nIn FlashInfer, we implement a unified strategy for data rep-\nresentation. Query and output matrices are efficiently stored\nas ragged tensors (also known as jagged arrays) (Tensor-\nflow Developers, 2018) without padding, which facilitates\nthe compact packing of queries and outputs from diverse\nrequests into a single tensor. Initially, keys and values are\nmaintained in ragged tensors using the same index pointers\nas queries, as they originate from the projection matrices\nWq, Wk, Wv applied to the same input. These keys and val-\nues are subsequently incorporated into the KV-Cache with\nnewly updated entries. The KV-Cache employs a block-\nsparse row (BSR) format, where block sizes are defined\nby application requirements: Br corresponds to the query\ntile size, details of which will be discussed in later sec-\ntions, and Bc is specified by KV-Cache management algo-\nrithms. FlashInfer kernel implementations supports arbitrary\n(Br, Bc) values.\nBlock size\n(1, 1)\nBlock size\n(1, 1)\nBlock size\n(3, 1)\n1\n2\n3\n4\n5\n6\n1\n2\n3\n4\n5\n6\n1\n2\n ThreadBlock\nIndex\nqueries\nGlobal Memory\nL2 Cache\nSMEM\nReg\nComposable Formats for\nMemory-Efficient Computation\nAttention Kernels with large block size, requests\n inside the same block access common KV-Cache\n(e.g. shared-prefix) via Shared Memory/Registers\nAttention Kernels with small block size, \naccess KV-Cache through Global Memory/L2 Cache.\nUnique\nKV-Cache\nShared\nKV-Cache\nThroughput\nFigure 3. Composable formats for shared-prefix decomposition in\nattention computation. The queries corresponding to the first 6\nrows have a shared prefix, as do the queries in the last 6 rows.\nWe store the KV cache corresponding to the shared prefix in a\nblock sparse matrix with a block size of (3, 1), while storing the\nremaining unique KV cache in another block sparse matrix with\na block size of (1, 1). For block size (3, 1), 3 queries can share\nthe same KV cache in high-bandwidth shared memory, while for\nblock size (1, 1), each query access KV-Cache within its own\nthreadblock, which can only go through low-bandwidth global\nmemory or L2 cache.\n3.1.2\nComposable Formats for Memory Efficiency\nInspired by SparseTIR (Ye et al., 2023), we enhance atten-\ntion computation efficiency through composable formats.\nThis approach leverages multiple block sparse formats in-\nstead of a single format to store the sparse matrix, offering\ngreater flexibility and memory efficiency. Single block-\nsparse formats are constrained by a fixed block size, limiting\nmemory efficiency based on the number of rows in the block\n(Br). While larger Br values improve shared memory and\nregister reuse for requests within the same block, they also\n\n\n=== PDF page 5 ===\nFlashInfer: Efficient and Customizable Attention Engine for LLM Inference Serving\nincrease fragmentation. Conversely, requests in different\nblocks cannot access each other’s shared memory.\nOur composable format design allows for the decomposition\nof the KV cache sparse matrix based on prior knowledge.\nFor instance, if certain requests share a prefix, the corre-\nsponding rows and columns in the KV cache form a dense\nsubmatrix. We can then use a block sparse matrix with a\nlarger Br to store these submatrices efficiently. Figure 3\nillustrates this concept, showing how shared prefixes can\nbe optimized using composable formats. This approach\ndoesn’t require data movement in the KV cache; instead,\nwe compute the indices and index pointer arrays for the\nsparse submatrices. Attention computations on larger block\nsizes can access shared KV cache entries using fast shared\nmemory and registers, significantly improving memory effi-\nciency.\n3.2\nCompute Abstraction\nWe developed CUDA/CUTLASS (Thakkar et al., 2023)\ntemplates for FlashAttention, designed specifically for\nboth dense and block-sparse matrices and compatible with\nNVIDIA GPU architectures from Turing to Hopper (sm75\nto sm90a). Our implementations utilize the FlashAttention2\n(FA2 for short) algorithm (Dao, 2023) for architectures up\nto Ada(sm89), and the FlashAttention3 (FA3 for short) al-\ngorithm (Shah et al., 2024) for Hopper. Key improvements\ninclude enhanced loading of sparse tiles into shared memory,\nexpanded tile-size configurations, optimized memory access\npatterns for grouped query attention, and customizable at-\ntention variants.\n3.2.1\nGlobal to Shared Memory Data Movement\nThe FlashInfer attention template supports any block size,\nrequiring a specialized data loading approach since blocks\nmay not align with tensor core shapes. As discussed in\nSection 2.3, we address these challenges by transferring\ntiles from scattered global memory to contiguous shared\nmemory for dense tensor core operations. Tensor core inputs\nfor a single MMA instruction can originate from different\nblocks within a block-sparse matrix. Figure 4 illustrates\nhow FlashInfer loads tiles from sparse/dense KV-Cache into\nshared memory; sparse KV-Cache addresses are computed\nusing the indices arrays of the BSR matrix, while dense\nones use row index affine transformations.\nThe last dimension of the KV-Cache remains contiguous\n(with size of head dimension d, commonly 128 or 256),\nmaintaining coalesced memory access that fits GPU cache\nline sizes. We use asynchronous copy instructions LDGSTS\nwith a 128B width to maximize memory bandwidth. Al-\nthough the Tensor Memory Accelerator (TMA) in Hopper\narchitecture can further accelerate data movement, it doesn’t\nsupport non-affine memory access patterns. Consequently,\nwe only use TMA for contiguous KV-Cache on Hopper\nGPUs and fall back to Ampere-style asynchronous copies\nfor other settings where TMA isn’t suitable.\nLoading\nSparse K/V tiles\n in sparse storage\n(Column Major)\n in dense storage\n(Column Major)\nLoading\nContiguous K/V tiles\nShared[i] <- Global[j]\nj=indices[(offset+i)/b_c]\n         +(offset+i)%b_c\nShared[i] <- Global[j]\nj=offset+i\nLDGSTS.128B\nLDGSTS.128B\nFigure 4. Data transfer from global to shared memory for sparse/-\ndense KV-Cache in FlashInfer.\nLeft: Sparse KV-Cache with\nbc = 2; Right: Dense KV-Cache. Head dimension d.\nPost-transfer to shared memory, the sparse and dense\nFlashAttention implementations converge, allowing con-\nsistent kernel usage with variations only in data loading\nmodules.\n3.2.2\nMicrokernel with Different Tile Sizes\nTo adapt to the varying operational intensities of LLM ap-\nplications, FlashInfer implements the FA2 algorithm across\nmultiple sizes. Traditional FA2 uses limited number of tile\nsizes (e.g., (128, 64)), optimal for prefill on A100 but ineffi-\ncient for shorter-query-length decoding. One architecture’s\nideal tile size may not suit others; for instance, Ada(sm89)\nhas limited shared memory, affecting SM occupancy with\nlarge tiles.\nFlashInfer\noffers\nFA2\nkernels\nwith\ntile\nsizes\n(1, 16, 32, 64, 128) × (32, 64, 128)\nand\nselects\nusing\nheuristics based on hardware resources and workload\nintensity:\n1. Determine average query length (for Grouped-Query\nAttention, the query length are fused with head group\ndimension, see Appendix A) per batch, choosing the\nminimal query tile size meeting or exceeding it.\n2. Formulate register and shared memory constraints as\nfunctions of K/V tile size, maximizing SM resource\noccupancy.\n3.2.3\nJIT Compiler for Attention Variants\nFor query tile size 1, we use CUDA Cores template since\ntensor core instruction m (minimum rows) is 16, and use\nTensor Cores for other query tile sizes. For FA3 we pro-\nvides row tile sizes that are multiples of 64, aligning with\nHopper’s WGMMA requirements. Tile sizes resolve at\n\n\n=== PDF page 6 ===\nFlashInfer: Efficient and Customizable Attention Engine for LLM Inference Serving\nspec_decl = r\"\"\"\ntemplate <typename Params_, typename KernelTraits_>\nstruct FlashSigmoid {\n  using Params = typename Params_;\n  using KernelTraits = typename KernelTraits_;\n  static constexpr bool use_softmax = false;\n  float scale, bias;\n  FlashSigmoid(const Params& params, int batch_idx, uint8_t*\nsmem_ptr) {\n    // Copy from CUDA constant memory to registers\n    scale = params.scale;\n    bias = params.bias;\n  }\n  ...\n  float LogitsTransform(const Params& params, float\nlogit_score, int batch_idx, int qo_idx, int kv_idx, int\nqo_head_idx, int kv_head_idx) {\n    return 1. / (1. + expf(-(logits_score * scale + bias)));\n  }\n};\n\"\"\"\nattn_spec = AttentionSpec(\n  \"FlashSigmoid\",\n  dtype_q, dtype_kv, dtype_o, idtype, head_dim, is_sparse,\n  additional_vars=[(\"scale\", \"float\"), (\"bias\", \"float\")],\n  additional_tensors=[],\n  spec_decl=spec_decl\n)\ntemplate <typename DTypeQ, typename DTypeKV, typename DTypeO,\ntypename IdType>\nstruct Params {\n  DTypeQ* q;\n  DTypeKV* k, v;\n  DTypeO* o;\n  float* lse;\n  IdType* qo_indptr, kv_indptr, kv_indices, kv_seq_lens;\n  ...\n  // (generated) additional vars\n  float scale;\n  float bias;\n};\ntemplate <typename AttentionSpec>\n__global__ KernelTemplate(\n  AttentionSpec::Params params) {\n  ...\n  // Init attention specification class.\n  AttentionSpec attn(params, batch_idx, smem_ptr);\n  ...\n  // Iterate over all elements inside the thread logits tile.\n  for (int i = 0;
